Specialist AI: JAH Presolved Unit Conversions Librarian (Signature)

JAH Presolved Unit Conversions Librarian (Signature) answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.PresolvedUnitConversionsSignatureTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Records

Miles to kilometres (8 mi) JAH-PSUCS-000001 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: miles to kilometres (8 mi). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. The mile-to-kilometre conversion is the most used length conversion in the English-speaking world, where road distances are still posted in miles and science uses kilometres. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

History

The international mile was defined as exactly 1,609.344 metres by the international yard and pound agreement of 1959, so the conversion factor is exact, not measured. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: miles to kilometres (8 mi). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. The mile-to-kilometre conversion is the most used length conversion in the English-speaking world, where road distances are still posted in miles and science uses kilometres. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

The Problem

Convert 8 miles to kilometres. 1 mile = 1.609344 km exactly. Give the answer in kilometres and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 miles = 1.609344 kilometres.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 8.0 miles x 1.609344 = 12.8748 kilometres.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 12.8748 / 1.609344 = 8.0000 miles.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 miles, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

8.0 miles is 12.87475 km. Converting back gives 8.00000 miles, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any distance in miles to kilometres, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for the international (statute) mile. The US survey mile differs by 2 parts per million and is now deprecated; nautical miles are a different unit altogether (1,852 m). Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 8.0, 'factor': 1.609344, 'from_u': 'miles', 'to_u': 'kilometres'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (miles):', P['value'])
print('Factor (km per mile):', P['factor'])
print('Kilometres: %.5f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to miles: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Value (miles): 8.0
Factor (km per mile): 1.609344
Kilometres: 12.87475
Back to miles: 8.00000
Round-trip error: 0.00e+00
Run 1 SHA-256: 7792d8862080fd4cdc525527880602e93497473276d54168a0043e373a076b87
Run 2 SHA-256: 7792d8862080fd4cdc525527880602e93497473276d54168a0043e373a076b87
Deterministic: MATCH

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Miles to kilometres (8 mi) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 8.0, 'factor': 1.609344, 'from_u': 'miles', 'to_u': 'kilometres'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (miles):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (km per mile):', P['factor'])
        print('Kilometres: %.5f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to miles: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Scale A1: Scale A1 — larger inputs: the same miles to kilometres with larger quantities, worked the same way and verified by the same demo.

Scale A3: Scale A3 — chained and combined: the miles to kilometres chained to the next step of the calculation, with the answer of this record as the input of the next, worked the same way.

Formal status

Scientific equation

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Miles to kilometres (8 mi) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 8.0, 'factor': 1.609344, 'from_u': 'miles', 'to_u': 'kilometres'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (miles):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (km per mile):', P['factor'])
        print('Kilometres: %.5f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to miles: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Kilometres to miles (25 km) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 25.0, 'factor': 0.621371192, 'from_u': 'kilometres', 'to_u': 'miles'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (km):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (miles per km):', P['factor'])
        print('Miles: %.5f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to km: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

The kilometre is 1,000 metres by definition, and the mile is 1,609.344 metres by the 1959 agreement, so the factor 0.621371192 is the exact reciprocal of 1.609344. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Problem

Convert 25 kilometres to miles. 1 km = 0.621371192 miles. Give the answer in miles and verify it by converting back.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 kilometres = 0.621371192 miles. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 25.0 kilometres x 0.621371192 = 15.5343 miles. Step 3: Check by converting back: 15.5343 / 0.621371192 = 25.0000 kilometres. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 kilometres, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Value and factor stated in the problem; 1 km = 0.621371192 miles.

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

Skeptical analysis

This holds only for the international mile. A rounded factor (0.62) is fine for rough estimates but not for precise work. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 25.0, 'factor': 0.621371192, 'from_u': 'kilometres', 'to_u': 'miles'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (km):', P['value'])
print('Factor (miles per km):', P['factor'])
print('Miles: %.5f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to km: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Kilometres to miles (25 km) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 25.0, 'factor': 0.621371192, 'from_u': 'kilometres', 'to_u': 'miles'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (km):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (miles per km):', P['factor'])
        print('Miles: %.5f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to km: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()

History

The Fahrenheit scale was defined by Daniel Fahrenheit in the early eighteenth century; the modern exact relation (a Fahrenheit degree is 5/9 of a Celsius degree, with water freezing at 32 F) was fixed when the scales were tied to the Celsius scale. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: celsius to fahrenheit (37 c). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Celsius to Fahrenheit is the everyday temperature conversion between the metric scale used in science and the scale still used in the United States. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

The Problem

Convert 37 degrees Celsius to Fahrenheit. The formula is F = C x 9/5 + 32. Give the answer in degrees Fahrenheit and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the formula: F = C x 9/5 + 32.
  2. Step 2: Multiply: 37.0 x 9/5 = 66.6.
  3. Step 3: Add 32: 66.6 + 32 = 98.60 degrees Fahrenheit.
  4. Step 4: Check by converting back: (98.60 - 32) x 5/9 = 37.00 degrees Celsius.
  5. Step 5: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

37.0 degrees Celsius is 98.60 degrees Fahrenheit. Converting back gives 37.00 degrees Celsius, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same formula converts any Celsius temperature to Fahrenheit, and the inverse formula converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for temperature values, not temperature differences: a difference of 1 Celsius degree is 1.8 Fahrenheit degrees, with no +32. The formula is exact as written. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication, one addition, and the inverse operations for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A temperature conversion between Celsius and Fahrenheit is a linear formula with an offset, not a pure multiplication: the degree sizes differ by 9/5 and the zero points differ by 32 degrees. The formula is exact as defined, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 37.0, 'from_u': 'degrees Celsius', 'to_u': 'degrees Fahrenheit'}
def solve(P):
    times = P['value'] * 9.0 / 5.0
    forward = times + 32.0
    reverse = (forward - 32.0) * 5.0 / 9.0
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error, 'times': times}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (C):', P['value'])
print('Fahrenheit: %.2f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to Celsius: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Celsius to Fahrenheit (37 C) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 37.0, 'from_u': 'degrees Celsius', 'to_u': 'degrees Fahrenheit'}
    def solve(self, P):
        times = P['value'] * 9.0 / 5.0
        forward = times + 32.0
        reverse = (forward - 32.0) * 5.0 / 9.0
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error, 'times': times}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (C):', P['value'])
        print('Fahrenheit: %.2f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to Celsius: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))

        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Fahrenheit to Celsius (212 F) JAH-PSUCS-000004 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: fahrenheit to celsius (212 f). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Fahrenheit to Celsius is the reverse of the Celsius conversion, used whenever an American temperature must be read on the metric scale. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

History

The Fahrenheit scale was defined by Daniel Fahrenheit in the early eighteenth century; the modern exact relation ties it to the Celsius scale with water freezing at 32 F and boiling at 212 F. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: fahrenheit to celsius (212 f). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Fahrenheit to Celsius is the reverse of the Celsius conversion, used whenever an American temperature must be read on the metric scale. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

The Problem

Convert 212 degrees Fahrenheit to Celsius. The formula is C = (F - 32) x 5/9. Give the answer in degrees Celsius and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the formula: C = (F - 32) x 5/9.
  2. Step 2: Subtract 32: 212.0 - 32 = 180.0.
  3. Step 3: Multiply by 5/9: 180.0 x 5/9 = 100.00 degrees Celsius.
  4. Step 4: Check by converting back: 100.00 x 9/5 + 32 = 212.00 degrees Fahrenheit.
  5. Step 5: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

212.0 degrees Fahrenheit is 100.00 degrees Celsius. Converting back gives 212.00 degrees Fahrenheit, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same formula converts any Fahrenheit temperature to Celsius, and the inverse formula converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for temperature values, not temperature differences: a difference of 1 Fahrenheit degree is 5/9 of a Celsius degree, with no -32. The formula is exact as written. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One subtraction, one multiplication, and the inverse operations for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A temperature conversion between Fahrenheit and Celsius is a linear formula with an offset, not a pure multiplication: subtract 32 first, then scale by 5/9. The formula is exact as defined, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 212.0, 'from_u': 'degrees Fahrenheit', 'to_u': 'degrees Celsius'}
def solve(P):
    minus = P['value'] - 32.0
    forward = minus * 5.0 / 9.0
    reverse = forward * 9.0 / 5.0 + 32.0
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error, 'minus': minus}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (F):', P['value'])
print('Celsius: %.2f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to Fahrenheit: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Fahrenheit to Celsius (212 F) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 212.0, 'from_u': 'degrees Fahrenheit', 'to_u': 'degrees Celsius'}
    def solve(self, P):
        minus = P['value'] - 32.0
        forward = minus * 5.0 / 9.0
        reverse = forward * 9.0 / 5.0 + 32.0
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error, 'minus': minus}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (F):', P['value'])
        print('Celsius: %.2f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to Fahrenheit: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it applies C = (F - 32) x 5/9 to the stated temperature, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Kelvin to Celsius (373.15 K) """

        reverse = forward + 273.15

        R2 = self.solve(P)

        print('Celsius: %.2f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to kelvin: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

History

The kelvin was named for Lord Kelvin, who proposed an absolute temperature scale in 1848; since 2019 the kelvin is defined by fixing the Boltzmann constant, and 273.15 K is exactly 0 C. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Problem

Convert 373.15 kelvin to degrees Celsius. The formula is C = K - 273.15. Give the answer in degrees Celsius and verify it by converting back.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the formula: C = K - 273.15. Step 2: Subtract: 373.15 - 273.15 = 100.00 degrees Celsius. Step 3: Check by converting back: 100.00 + 273.15 = 373.15 kelvin. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified. Step 5: Check the sense: 373.15 K is the boiling point of water, 100 C, and the answer matches. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Value stated in the problem; offset 273.15 exactly.

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it applies C = K - 273.15 to the stated temperature, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

Results reported

373.15 kelvin is 100.00 degrees Celsius. Converting back gives 373.15 kelvin, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds exactly as written, since the offset is exact by definition. It does not apply to temperature differences, which are the same number in kelvin and Celsius. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 373.15, 'from_u': 'kelvin', 'to_u': 'degrees Celsius'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] - 273.15
    reverse = forward + 273.15
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (K):', P['value'])
print('Celsius: %.2f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to kelvin: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Kelvin to Celsius (373.15 K) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 373.15, 'from_u': 'kelvin', 'to_u': 'degrees Celsius'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] - 273.15
        reverse = forward + 273.15
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (K):', P['value'])
        print('Celsius: %.2f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to kelvin: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Pounds to kilograms (150 lb) JAH-PSUCS-000006 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: pounds to kilograms (150 lb). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. Pounds to kilograms is the everyday mass conversion between the imperial system and the metric system, used for body weight, food, and freight. Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: pounds to kilograms (150 lb). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Pounds to kilograms is the everyday mass conversion between the imperial system and the metric system, used for body weight, food, and freight. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

The Problem

Convert 150 pounds to kilograms. 1 lb = 0.45359237 kg exactly. Give the answer in kilograms and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 pounds = 0.45359237 kilograms.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 150.0 pounds x 0.45359237 = 68.0389 kilograms.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 68.0389 / 0.45359237 = 150.0000 pounds.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 2.84e-14 pounds, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

150.0 lb is 68.03886 kg. Converting back gives 150.00000 lb, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any mass in pounds to kilograms, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for the avoirdupois pound, the ordinary pound. The troy pound (used for precious metals) is different, and pounds here mean mass, not force. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 150.0, 'factor': 0.45359237, 'from_u': 'pounds', 'to_u': 'kilograms'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)

_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Pounds to kilograms (150 lb) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 150.0, 'factor': 0.45359237, 'from_u': 'pounds', 'to_u': 'kilograms'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (lb):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (kg per lb):', P['factor'])
        print('Kilograms: %.5f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to lb: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: kilograms to pounds (70 kg). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Kilograms to pounds is the reverse of the pound conversion, used whenever a metric mass must be read in pounds. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

History

The kilogram was the SI unit of mass until 2019, when it was redefined by fixing the Planck constant; the pound relation 1 lb = 0.45359237 kg exactly gives the reciprocal factor used here. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: kilograms to pounds (70 kg). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. Kilograms to pounds is the reverse of the pound conversion, used whenever a metric mass must be read in pounds. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

The Problem

Convert 70 kilograms to pounds. 1 kg = 2.20462262 lb. Give the answer in pounds and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 kilograms = 2.20462262 pounds.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 70.0 kilograms x 2.20462262 = 154.3236 pounds.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 154.3236 / 2.20462262 = 70.0000 kilograms.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 kilograms, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

70.0 kg is 154.32358 lb. Converting back gives 70.00000 kg, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any mass in kilograms to pounds, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for the avoirdupois pound. A rounded factor (2.2) is fine for rough estimates but not for precise work. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 70.0, 'factor': 2.20462262, 'from_u': 'kilograms', 'to_u': 'pounds'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (kg):', P['value'])
print('Factor (lb per kg):', P['factor'])
print('Pounds: %.5f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to kg: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Value (kg): 70.0
Factor (lb per kg): 2.20462262
Pounds: 154.32358
Back to kg: 70.00000
Round-trip error: 0.00e+00
Run 1 SHA-256: db2607320a73c6bae83cd9526e19adc91b545be7831283e64d7df9534cd2f54e
Run 2 SHA-256: db2607320a73c6bae83cd9526e19adc91b545be7831283e64d7df9534cd2f54e
Deterministic: MATCH

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Kilograms to pounds (70 kg) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 70.0, 'factor': 2.20462262, 'from_u': 'kilograms', 'to_u': 'pounds'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (kg):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (lb per kg):', P['factor'])
        print('Pounds: %.5f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to kg: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

import math

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Litres to US gallons (20 L) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 20.0, 'factor': 0.264172052, 'from_u': 'litres', 'to_u': 'US gallons'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (L):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (gal per L):', P['factor'])
        print('US gallons: %.5f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to litres: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 20.0, 'factor': 0.264172052, 'from_u': 'litres', 'to_u': 'US gallons'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (L):', P['value'])
print('Factor (gal per L):', P['factor'])
print('US gallons: %.5f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to litres: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Full working program

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Litres to US gallons (20 L) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 20.0, 'factor': 0.264172052, 'from_u': 'litres', 'to_u': 'US gallons'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (L):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (gal per L):', P['factor'])
        print('US gallons: %.5f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to litres: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

The Problem

Convert 12 US gallons to litres. 1 US gallon = 3.785411784 L exactly. Give the answer in litres and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 US gallons = 3.785411784 litres.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 12.0 US gallons x 3.785411784 = 45.4249 litres.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 45.4249 / 3.785411784 = 12.0000 US gallons.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 1.78e-15 US gallons, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

12.0 US gallons is 45.42494 L. Converting back gives 12.00000 US gallons, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any volume in US gallons to litres, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for the US gallon. The imperial gallon (4.54609 L) is about 20 percent larger, and the two must never be mixed. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: US gallons to litres (12 gal) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 12.0, 'factor': 3.785411784, 'from_u': 'US gallons', 'to_u': 'litres'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (gal):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (L per gal):', P['factor'])
        print('Litres: %.5f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to gallons: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Inches to centimetres is the basic length conversion between the imperial and metric systems, used for screen sizes, paper, and body measurements. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

The Problem

Convert 36 inches to centimetres. 1 inch = 2.54 cm exactly. Give the answer in centimetres and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 inches = 2.54 centimetres.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 36.0 inches x 2.54 = 91.4400 centimetres.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 91.4400 / 2.54 = 36.0000 inches.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 inches, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

36.0 inches is 91.44 cm. Converting back gives 36.00 inches, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any length in inches to centimetres, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds exactly as written, since the factor is exact by definition. It does not apply to the historical inches of other countries, which differed before 1959. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 36.0, 'factor': 2.54, 'from_u': 'inches', 'to_u': 'centimetres'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (in):', P['value'])
print('Factor (cm per in):', P['factor'])
print('Centimetres: %.2f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to inches: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Value (in): 36.0
Factor (cm per in): 2.54
Centimetres: 91.44
Back to inches: 36.00
Round-trip error: 0.00e+00
Run 1 SHA-256: c1ba559cbcf5f7b3a52ba2598526c9fbc5057b19e19e4c0398e614624a64c813
Run 2 SHA-256: c1ba559cbcf5f7b3a52ba2598526c9fbc5057b19e19e4c0398e614624a64c813
Deterministic: MATCH

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Inches to centimetres (36 in) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 36.0, 'factor': 2.54, 'from_u': 'inches', 'to_u': 'centimetres'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (in):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (cm per in):', P['factor'])
        print('Centimetres: %.2f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to inches: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

The inch was defined as exactly 2.54 centimetres by the international yard and pound agreement of 1959, so the conversion factor is exact. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

import math
P = {'value': 10.0, 'factor': 0.3048, 'from_u': 'feet', 'to_u': 'metres'}

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Feet to metres (10 ft) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 10.0, 'factor': 0.3048, 'from_u': 'feet', 'to_u': 'metres'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (ft):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (m per ft):', P['factor'])
        print('Metres: %.4f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to feet: %.4f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

10.0 feet is 3.0480 m. Converting back gives 10.0000 feet, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only for the international foot. The US survey foot differs by 2 parts per million and is now deprecated; historical feet differed more. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Specification

  • care: Confirm the foot is the international foot; the US survey foot is deprecated and differs slightly.
  • dimensions: {'computed error': '0.0', 'computed forward': '3.048', 'computed reverse': '10.0', 'factor': '0.3048', 'from_u': 'feet', 'to_u': 'metres', 'value': '10.0'}
