Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 10.0, 'factor': 0.3048, 'from_u': 'feet', 'to_u': 'metres'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (ft):', P['value'])
print('Factor (m per ft):', P['factor'])
print('Metres: %.4f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to feet: %.4f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Feet to metres (10 ft) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 10.0, 'factor': 0.3048, 'from_u': 'feet', 'to_u': 'metres'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (ft):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (m per ft):', P['factor'])
        print('Metres: %.4f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to feet: %.4f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: square metres to square feet (50 m2). History

The factor is the square of the length factor: one metre is 3.280839895 feet, so one square metre is 3.280839895 squared, or 10.7639104, square feet. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: square metres to square feet (50 m2). The Problem

Convert 50 square metres to square feet. 1 m2 = 10.7639104 sq ft. Give the answer in square feet and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 square metres = 10.7639104 square feet.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 50.0 square metres x 10.7639104 = 538.1955 square feet.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 538.1955 / 10.7639104 = 50.0000 square metres.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 square metres, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

50.0 square metres is 538.196 square feet. Converting back gives 50.000 square metres, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any area in square metres to square feet, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only with the international foot. An area factor is the square of the length factor, so it must never be confused with the length factor itself. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Square metres to square feet (50 m2) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 50.0, 'factor': 10.7639104, 'from_u': 'square metres', 'to_u': 'square feet'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (m2):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (sq ft per m2):', P['factor'])
        print('Square feet: %.3f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to m2: %.3f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Limits

This holds only for the international acre. Historical acres varied by country, and the Scottish acre was larger; modern land records use the international acre. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 100.0, 'factor': 0.404685642, 'from_u': 'acres', 'to_u': 'hectares'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (acres):', P['value'])
print('Factor (ha per acre):', P['factor'])
print('Hectares: %.5f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to acres: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Run 2 SHA-256: f92cc3803d8475772083c2050dfcdd2c7ced51e0f7ebbf0923f0f4e625dcec56

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Acres to hectares (100 acres) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 100.0, 'factor': 0.404685642, 'from_u': 'acres', 'to_u': 'hectares'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (acres):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (ha per acre):', P['factor'])
        print('Hectares: %.5f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to acres: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

The international acre is defined as exactly 4,046.8564224 square metres, and a hectare is 10,000 square metres, so one acre is exactly 0.40468564224 hectares. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Problem

Convert 100 acres to hectares. 1 acre = 0.404685642 ha. Give the answer in hectares and verify it by converting back.

Method

Step 3: Check by converting back: 40.4686 / 0.404685642 = 100.0000 acres. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 acres, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Problem

The Worked Solution

  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 5.0 hours x 3600.0 = 18000.0000 seconds.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 18000.0000 / 3600.0 = 5.0000 hours.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 hours, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

When It Applies

It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Hours to seconds (5 h) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 5.0, 'factor': 3600.0, 'from_u': 'hours', 'to_u': 'seconds'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (hours):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (s per hour):', P['factor'])
        print('Seconds: %.1f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to hours: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 5.0, 'factor': 3600.0, 'from_u': 'hours', 'to_u': 'seconds'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (hours):', P['value'])
print('Factor (s per hour):', P['factor'])
print('Seconds: %.1f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to hours: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Hours to seconds (5 h) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 5.0, 'factor': 3600.0, 'from_u': 'hours', 'to_u': 'seconds'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (hours):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (s per hour):', P['factor'])
        print('Seconds: %.1f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to hours: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Days to minutes (7 days) JAH-PSUCS-000015 · simple

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: days to minutes (7 days). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Days to minutes is the time conversion used for schedules, trips, and durations counted in days. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

History

A day is 24 hours, an hour is 60 minutes, so one day is exactly 1,440 minutes; the day is the civil unit tied to Earth's rotation. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: days to minutes (7 days). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Days to minutes is the time conversion used for schedules, trips, and durations counted in days. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

The Problem

Convert 7 days to minutes. 1 day = 1,440 minutes. Give the answer in minutes and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 days = 1440.0 minutes.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 7.0 days x 1440.0 = 10080.0000 minutes.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 10080.0000 / 1440.0 = 7.0000 days.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 days, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

7.0 days is 10080.0 minutes. Converting back gives 7.00000 days, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any duration in days to minutes, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds exactly as written for the standard 24-hour day. It does not apply across daylight-saving transitions, where a civil day can be 23 or 25 hours. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 7.0, 'factor': 1440.0, 'from_u': 'days', 'to_u': 'minutes'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}

_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Days to minutes (7 days) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 7.0, 'factor': 1440.0, 'from_u': 'days', 'to_u': 'minutes'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (days):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (min per day):', P['factor'])
        print('Minutes: %.1f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to days: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Bytes to kilobytes (1,048,576 B) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 1048576.0, 'factor': 0.0009765625, 'from_u': 'bytes', 'to_u': 'kilobytes'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (bytes):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (KB per byte):', P['factor'])
        print('Kilobytes: %.2f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to bytes: %.1f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

The binary kilobyte is 1,024 bytes (2 to the 10th power), the convention of computing since the 1960s; the factor used here is 1/1024. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Problem

Convert 1,048,576 bytes to kilobytes, using 1 kilobyte = 1,024 bytes. Give the answer in kilobytes and verify it by converting back.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 bytes = 0.0009765625 kilobytes. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 1048576.0 bytes x 0.0009765625 = 1024.0000 kilobytes. Step 3: Check by converting back: 1024.0000 / 0.0009765625 = 1048576.0000 bytes. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 bytes, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Value and factor stated in the problem; 1 kilobyte = 1,024 bytes (binary).

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

1 KB = 1024 B

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

1048576 bytes is 1024.00 kilobytes. Converting back gives 1048576.0 bytes, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only for the binary kilobyte (1,024 bytes). The decimal kilobyte used by some storage makers is 1,000 bytes, and the IEC name for 1,024 bytes is the kibibyte (KiB). Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: megabits per second to megabytes per second (1000 mbps). The Problem

Convert 1,000 megabits per second (Mbps) to megabytes per second (MB/s). 1 byte = 8 bits. Give the answer in MB/s and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 megabits per second = 0.125 megabytes per second.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 1000.0 megabits per second x 0.125 = 125.0000 megabytes per second.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 125.0000 / 0.125 = 1000.0000 megabits per second.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 megabits per second, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

1000 Mbps is 125.00 MB/s. Converting back gives 1000.00 Mbps, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any bit rate to the corresponding byte rate, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for the raw rate conversion. Real download speeds are lower because of protocol overhead, and the decimal megabit (1,000,000 bits) is the standard for network rates. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 1000.0, 'factor': 0.125, 'from_u': 'megabits per second', 'to_u': 'megabytes per second'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (Mbps):', P['value'])
print('Factor (MB/s per Mbps):', P['factor'])
print('Megabytes per second: %.2f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to Mbps: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Megabits per second to megabytes per second (1000 Mbps) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 1000.0, 'factor': 0.125, 'from_u': 'megabits per second', 'to_u': 'megabytes per second'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (Mbps):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (MB/s per Mbps):', P['factor'])
        print('Megabytes per second: %.2f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to Mbps: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Full working program

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Megabits per second to megabytes per second (1000 Mbps) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 1000.0, 'factor': 0.125, 'from_u': 'megabits per second', 'to_u': 'megabytes per second'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (Mbps):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (MB/s per Mbps):', P['factor'])
        print('Megabytes per second: %.2f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to Mbps: %.2f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Atmospheres to pascals (2.5 atm) JAH-PSUCS-000018 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: atmospheres to pascals (2.5 atm). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Atmospheres to pascals is the pressure conversion between the traditional atmosphere unit and the SI unit of pressure. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

History

The standard atmosphere was defined as exactly 101,325 pascals in 1954, matching the average sea-level pressure; the pascal itself is one newton per square metre. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: atmospheres to pascals (2.5 atm). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Atmospheres to pascals is the pressure conversion between the traditional atmosphere unit and the SI unit of pressure. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

The Problem

Convert 2.5 atmospheres to pascals. 1 atm = 101,325 Pa exactly. Give the answer in pascals and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 atmospheres = 101325.0 pascals.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 2.5 atmospheres x 101325.0 = 253312.5000 pascals.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 253312.5000 / 101325.0 = 2.5000 atmospheres.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 atmospheres, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

2.5 atm is 253312.5 Pa. Converting back gives 2.50000 atm, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any pressure in atmospheres to pascals, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds exactly as written, since the atmosphere is defined as 101,325 Pa. It does not apply to the technical atmosphere (at), which is 98,066.5 Pa, or to bar, which is 100,000 Pa. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 2.5, 'factor': 101325.0, 'from_u': 'atmospheres', 'to_u': 'pascals'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (atm):', P['value'])
print('Factor (Pa per atm):', P['factor'])
print('Pascals: %.1f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to atm: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Atmospheres to pascals (2.5 atm) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 2.5, 'factor': 101325.0, 'from_u': 'atmospheres', 'to_u': 'pascals'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (atm):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (Pa per atm):', P['factor'])
        print('Pascals: %.1f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to atm: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Joules to calories (1000 J) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 1000.0, 'factor': 0.239005736, 'from_u': 'joules', 'to_u': 'calories'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (J):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (cal per J):', P['factor'])
        print('Calories: %.3f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to joules: %.3f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

The thermochemical calorie is defined as exactly 4.184 joules; the factor used here is its reciprocal, 0.239005736 calories per joule. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Problem

Convert 1,000 joules to calories. 1 calorie = 4.184 J exactly (thermochemical calorie). Give the answer in calories and verify it by converting back.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 joules = 0.239005736 calories. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 1000.0 joules x 0.239005736 = 239.0057 calories. Step 3: Check by converting back: 239.0057 / 0.239005736 = 1000.0000 joules. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 joules, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Value and factor stated in the problem; 1 thermochemical calorie = 4.184 J exactly.

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

1000 J is 239.006 calories. Converting back gives 1000.000 J, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only for the thermochemical calorie (4.184 J). The International Table calorie is 4.1868 J, and the food Calorie (kilocalorie) is 1,000 calories; the three must never be mixed. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: horsepower to watts (5 hp). The Problem

Convert 5 horsepower to watts. 1 mechanical horsepower = 745.7 W. Give the answer in watts and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 horsepower = 745.7 watts.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 5.0 horsepower x 745.7 = 3728.5000 watts.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 3728.5000 / 745.7 = 5.0000 horsepower.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 horsepower, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

5 hp is 3728.5 W. Converting back gives 5.00000 hp, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any power in mechanical horsepower to watts, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for mechanical horsepower. Metric horsepower is 735.49875 W, and electrical horsepower is 746 W; the three differ slightly and must not be mixed. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Horsepower to watts (5 hp) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 5.0, 'factor': 745.7, 'from_u': 'horsepower', 'to_u': 'watts'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (hp):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (W per hp):', P['factor'])
        print('Watts: %.1f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to hp: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Horsepower to watts (5 hp) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 5.0, 'factor': 745.7, 'from_u': 'horsepower', 'to_u': 'watts'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (hp):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (W per hp):', P['factor'])
        print('Watts: %.1f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to hp: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Knots to kilometres per hour (25 kn) JAH-PSUCS-000021 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: knots to kilometres per hour (25 kn). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Knots to kilometres per hour is the speed conversion used in aviation and sailing, where speeds are given in knots and road speeds in km/h. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

History

The knot is one nautical mile per hour, and the nautical mile is defined as exactly 1,852 metres, so one knot is exactly 1.852 km/h. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: knots to kilometres per hour (25 kn). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Knots to kilometres per hour is the speed conversion used in aviation and sailing, where speeds are given in knots and road speeds in km/h. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

The Problem

Convert 25 knots to kilometres per hour. 1 knot = 1.852 km/h exactly. Give the answer in km/h and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 knots = 1.852 kilometres per hour.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 25.0 knots x 1.852 = 46.3000 kilometres per hour.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 46.3000 / 1.852 = 25.0000 knots.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 knots, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

25 knots is 46.30 km/h. Converting back gives 25.00000 knots, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any speed in knots to km/h, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds exactly as written, since the nautical mile is defined as 1,852 m. It does not apply to miles per hour, which use the statute mile instead. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Knots to kilometres per hour (25 kn) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 25.0, 'factor': 1.852, 'from_u': 'knots', 'to_u': 'kilometres per hour'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (kn):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (km/h per kn):', P['factor'])
        print('Kilometres per hour: %.2f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to knots: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Ounces to grams (16 oz) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 16.0, 'factor': 28.3495231, 'from_u': 'ounces', 'to_u': 'grams'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (oz):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (g per oz):', P['factor'])
        print('Grams: %.3f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to ounces: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

The avoirdupois ounce is one sixteenth of the international pound (0.45359237 kg), so one ounce is 28.349523125 grams exactly. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Problem

Convert 16 ounces to grams. 1 oz = 28.3495231 g. Give the answer in grams and verify it by converting back.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 ounces = 28.3495231 grams. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 16.0 ounces x 28.3495231 = 453.5924 grams. Step 3: Check by converting back: 453.5924 / 28.3495231 = 16.0000 ounces. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 ounces, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only for the avoirdupois ounce. The troy ounce (31.1034768 g) is used for precious metals, and fluid ounces measure volume, not mass. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 16.0, 'factor': 28.3495231, 'from_u': 'ounces', 'to_u': 'grams'}
def solve(P):
    forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
    reverse = forward / P['factor']
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (oz):', P['value'])
print('Factor (g per oz):', P['factor'])
print('Grams: %.3f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to ounces: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Ounces to grams (16 oz) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 16.0, 'factor': 28.3495231, 'from_u': 'ounces', 'to_u': 'grams'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (oz):', P['value'])
        print('Factor (g per oz):', P['factor'])
        print('Grams: %.3f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to ounces: %.5f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

Cups to millilitres (4 cups) JAH-PSUCS-000023 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: cups to millilitres (4 cups). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Cups to millilitres is the cooking volume conversion between US customary cups and the metric system. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

History

Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: cups to millilitres (4 cups). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Cups to millilitres is the cooking volume conversion between US customary cups and the metric system. Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

The Problem

Convert 4 US cups to millilitres. 1 US cup = 236.5882365 mL. Give the answer in millilitres and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the conversion factor: 1 US cups = 236.5882365 millilitres.
  2. Step 2: Multiply the value by the factor: 4.0 US cups x 236.5882365 = 946.3529 millilitres.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 946.3529 / 236.5882365 = 4.0000 US cups.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 0.00e+00 US cups, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the size: the answer is in the expected range for this conversion, and the units on both sides agree.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

4 US cups is 946.353 mL. Converting back gives 4.00000 US cups, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same factor converts any volume in US cups to millilitres, and the inverse factor converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for the US customary cup. The metric cup is 250 mL, the imperial cup is 284.13 mL, and the three must never be mixed in a recipe. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Cups to millilitres (4 cups) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 4.0, 'factor': 236.5882365, 'from_u': 'US cups', 'to_u': 'millilitres'}
    def solve(self, P):
        forward = P['value'] * P['factor']
        reverse = forward / P['factor']
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])

    def run(self):

        R2 = self.solve(P)

        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
