Radians to degrees (pi rad) JAH-PSUCS-000024 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: radians to degrees (pi rad). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Radians to degrees is the angle conversion between the mathematician's unit (radians) and the everyday unit (degrees). Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

History

The radian is defined so that a full circle is 2 pi radians, which is 360 degrees, so one radian is 180/pi degrees; the definition goes back to the eighteenth century. The units involved belong to the standard systems every AI must move between: the SI system and the imperial and US customary units still in everyday use. Conversion factors used here are the exact defined values (or the accepted standard values where a unit is defined by a physical standard), checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers measurement questions should not have to look up a factor and redo the arithmetic each time; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked unit-conversion problem: radians to degrees (pi rad). It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives the conversion factor and the method, works the arithmetic step by step, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the conversion from the same inputs in code and then converts back, so the result is verified by a round-trip check rather than taken on trust. Radians to degrees is the angle conversion between the mathematician's unit (radians) and the everyday unit (degrees). Unit conversions are the most frequently needed presolved lookup in practice, and the cheapest to verify perfectly: forward computation plus a reverse check. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Unit Conversions Database, each covering a different conversion, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked conversion ready-made.

The Problem

Convert pi radians (3.14159265359 rad) to degrees. The formula is degrees = radians x 180 / pi. Give the answer in degrees and verify it by converting back.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the formula: degrees = radians x 180 / pi.
  2. Step 2: Multiply: 3.14159265359 x 180 / pi = 180.0000 degrees.
  3. Step 3: Check by converting back: 180.0000 x pi / 180 = 3.141593 radians.
  4. Step 4: The round-trip error is 4.44e-16, which is zero within rounding, so the conversion is verified.
  5. Step 5: Check the sense: pi radians is a half turn, which is 180 degrees, and the answer matches.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

3.14159265359 radians is 180.0000 degrees. Converting back gives 3.141593 radians, confirming the conversion. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conversion is needed: the same from-unit, the same to-unit, and values inside the normal range. The same formula converts any angle in radians to degrees, and the inverse formula converts back. It applies to everyday measurement questions, laboratory and engineering arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where a different unit definition is in force; see Limits.

Limits

This holds exactly as written. It does not apply to grads (400 per circle) or turns (1 per circle), which use different full-circle values. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated units and the stated factor; a different unit system, a different definition (for example US survey foot versus international foot, or imperial versus US gallons), or a temperature conversion that needs an offset rather than a pure factor, needs its own worked solution. The record never claims more than its inputs support, and where a factor is rounded the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs and does not generalise itself.

Math Proof

One multiplication (or one linear formula for temperature) and one division for the reverse check, evaluated in code by the demo, with the round-trip error printed. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the conversion itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, the reverse conversion recovers the input within rounding, and a factor applied the wrong way round would show up at once in the round-trip error.

Science Proof

A unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition or a standard value cited to the references, so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The solution is scientifically sound because a unit conversion is a multiplication by a defined ratio: the factor is exact by definition (or a standard value cited to the references), so the only possible error is arithmetic, and the demo removes that by computing in code and checking the round trip. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'value': 3.14159265359, 'from_u': 'radians', 'to_u': 'degrees'}
def solve(P):
    import math as _m
    forward = P['value'] * 180.0 / _m.pi
    reverse = forward * _m.pi / 180.0
    error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
    return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Value (rad):', P['value'])
print('Degrees: %.4f' % R['forward'])
print('Back to radians: %.6f' % R['reverse'])
print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Value (rad): 3.14159265359
Degrees: 180.0000
Back to radians: 3.141593
Round-trip error: 4.44e-16
Run 1 SHA-256: 21b7e87588f569b607580497d63fc4275ce4663e04a0ef2b5a6585553447f138
Run 2 SHA-256: 21b7e87588f569b607580497d63fc4275ce4663e04a0ef2b5a6585553447f138
Deterministic: MATCH

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Radians to degrees (pi rad) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 3.14159265359, 'from_u': 'radians', 'to_u': 'degrees'}
    def solve(self, P):
        import math as _m
        forward = P['value'] * 180.0 / _m.pi
        reverse = forward * _m.pi / 180.0
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])
        return {'forward': forward, 'reverse': reverse, 'error': error}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Value (rad):', P['value'])
        print('Degrees: %.4f' % R['forward'])
        print('Back to radians: %.6f' % R['reverse'])
        print('Round-trip error: %.2e' % R['error'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Formal equation, in plain words

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo computes the forward conversion from the problem's own inputs: it computes the forward conversion from the stated value and factor, converts the answer back, and prints the round-trip error. Second, the demo converts the answer back and compares it with the original input; the round-trip error is printed and must be zero within rounding. Third, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Radians to degrees (pi rad) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'value': 3.14159265359, 'from_u': 'radians', 'to_u': 'degrees'}
    def solve(self, P):

        reverse = forward * _m.pi / 180.0
        error = abs(reverse - P['value'])

        R2 = self.solve(P)

        print('Degrees: %.4f' % R['forward'])

        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
